Connectivity Labeling in Faulty Colored Graphs
Authors: Asaf Petruschka, Shay Sapir, Elad Tzalik
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
Fault-tolerant connectivity labelings are schemes that, given an n-vertex graph \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G=(V,E)$$\end{document}G=(V,E) and a parameter f, produce succinct yet informative labels for the elements of the graph. Given only the labels of two vertices u, v and of the elements in a faulty-set F with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vert F\vert \le f$$\end{document}|F|≤f, one can determine if u, v are connected in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G-F$$\end{document}G-F, the surviving graph after removing F. For the edge or vertex faults models, i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F\subseteq E$$\end{document}F⊆E or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F \subseteq V$$\end{document}F⊆V, a sequence of recent work established schemes with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {poly}(f,\log n)$$\end{document}poly(f,logn)-bit labels for general graphs. This paper considers the color faults model, recently introduced in the context of spanners [Petruschka, Sapir and Tzalik, ITCS ’24], which accounts for known correlations between failures. Here, the edges (or vertices) of the input G are arbitrarily colored, and the faulty elements in F are colors; a failing color causes all edges (vertices) of that color to crash. While treating color faults by naïvly applying solutions for many failing edges or vertices is inefficient, the known correlations could potentially be exploited to provide better solutions. Our main contribution is settling the label length complexity for connectivity under one color fault (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f=1$$\end{document}f=1). The existing implicit solution, by black-box application of the state-of-the-art scheme for edge faults of [Dory and Parter, PODC ’21], might yield labels of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega (n)$$\end{document}Ω(n) bits. We provide a deterministic scheme with labels of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(\sqrt{n})$$\end{document}O~(n) bits in the worst case, and a matching lower bound. Moreover, our scheme is universally optimal: even schemes tailored to handle only colorings of one specific graph topology (i.e., may store the topology “for free”) cannot produce asymptotically smaller labels. We characterize the optimal length by a new graph parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{bp}(G)$$\end{document}bp(G) called the ball packing number. We further extend our labeling approach to yield a routing scheme avoiding a single forbidden color, with routing tables of size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(\textsf{bp}(G))$$\end{document}O~(bp(G)) bits. We also consider the centralized setting, and show an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(n)$$\end{document}O~(n)-space oracle, answering connectivity queries under one color fault in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(1)$$\end{document}O~(1) time. Curiously, by our results, no oracle with such space can be evenly distributed as labels. Turning to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f\ge 2$$\end{document}f≥2 color faults, we give a randomized labeling scheme with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(n^{1-1/2^f})$$\end{document}O~(n1-1/2f)-bit labels, along with a lower bound of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega (n^{1-1/(f+1)})$$\end{document}Ω(n1-1/(f+1)) bits. For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f=2$$\end{document}f=2, we make partial improvement by providing labels of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(\textrm{diam}(G)\sqrt{n})$$\end{document}O~(diam(G)n) bits, and show that this scheme is (nearly) optimal when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{diam}(G)=\tilde{O}(1)$$\end{document}diam(G)=O~(1). Additionally, we present a general reduction from the above all-pairs formulation of fault-tolerant connectivity labeling (in any fault model) to the single-source variant, which could also be applicable for centralized oracles, streaming, or dynamic algorithms.

Introduction

Labeling schemes are important distributed graph data structures with diverse applications in graph algorithms and distributed computing, concerned with assigning the vertices of a graph (and possibly also other elements, such as edges) with succinct yet informative labels. Many real-life networks are often error-prone by nature, which motivates the study of fault-tolerant graph structures and services. In a fault-tolerant connectivity labeling scheme, we are given an n-vertex graph \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G=(V,E)$$\end{document}G=(V,E) and an integer f, and should assign short labels to the elements of G, such that the following holds: For every pair of vertices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u,v \in V$$\end{document}u,v∈V and faulty-set F with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vert F\vert \le f$$\end{document}|F|≤f, one can determine if u and v are connected in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G-F$$\end{document}G-F by merely inspecting the labels of the elements in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{u,v\}\cup F$$\end{document}{u,v}∪F. The main complexity measure is the maximal label length (in bits), while construction and query time are secondary measures.

The concept of edge/vertex-fault-tolerant labeling, aka forbidden set labeling, was explicitly introduced by Courcelle and Twigg [1]. Earlier work on fault-tolerant connectivity and distance labeling focused on graph families such as planar graphs and graphs with bounded treewidth or doubling dimension [1–4]. Up until recently, designing edge- or vertex-fault-tolerant connectivity labels for general graphs remained fairly open. Dory and Parter [5] were the first to construct randomized labeling schemes for connectivity under f edge faults, where a query is answered correctly with high probability,1 with length of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(\min \{f+\log n,\log ^3 n\})$$\end{document}O(min{f+logn,log3n}) bits. Izumi, Emek, Wadayama and Masuzawa [6] derandomized this construction, showing deterministic labels of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(f^2)$$\end{document}O~(f2) bits.2 Turning to f vertex faults, Parter and Petruschka [7] were the first to design connectivity labels for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f\le 2$$\end{document}f≤2 with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(1)$$\end{document}O~(1) bits. Subsequently, Parter, Petruschka and Pettie [8] provided a randomized scheme for any f vertex faults with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(f^3)$$\end{document}O~(f3) bits and a derandomized version with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(f^7)$$\end{document}O~(f7) bits, along with a lower bound of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega (f)$$\end{document}Ω(f) bits. Very recently, Long, Pettie and Saranurak [9] improved the state-of-the-art bounds in all settings by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {poly}(f, \log n)$$\end{document}poly(f,logn) factors: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(\min \{f + \log n, \log ^2 n \log f\})$$\end{document}O(min{f+logn,log2nlogf}) randomized or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(\sqrt{f})$$\end{document}O~(f) deterministic for edge faults, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{O(f^2)}$$\end{document}O(f2)~ randomized or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(f^4)$$\end{document}O~(f4) deterministic for vertex faults. Another important research area which is closely related to fault-tolerant labeling concerns the design of forbidden-set routing schemes, see e.g. [1, 3–5, 8]. (Further background on such routing schemes is given in Section 1.1.1.)

In this work, we consider labeling schemes for connectivity under color faults, a model that was very recently introduced in the context of graph spanners [10], which intuitively accounts for known correlations between failures. In this model, the edges or vertices of the input graph G are arbitrarily partitioned into classes, or equivalently, associated with colors, and a set F of f such color classes might fail. A failing color causes all edges (vertices) of that color to crash. The surviving subgraph \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G-F$$\end{document}G-F is formed by deleting every edge or vertex with color from F. (In the edge-colored case we naturally allow multi-graphs where parallel edges may have different colors.) The scheme must assign labels to the vertices and to the colors of G, so that a connectivity query \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\langle u,v,F \right\rangle $$\end{document}u,v,F can be answered by inspecting only the labels of the vertices u, v and of the colors in F.

This new notion generalizes edge/vertex fault-tolerant schemes, that are obtained in the special case when each edge or vertex has a unique color. However, in the general case, even a single color fault may correspond to many and arbitrarily spread edge/vertex faults, which poses a major challenge. Tackling this issue by naively applying the existing solutions for many individual edge/vertex faults (i.e., by letting the label of a color store all labels given to elements in its class) may result in very large labels of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega (n)$$\end{document}Ω(n) bits or more, even when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f=1$$\end{document}f=1. On a high level, this work shows that the correlation between the faulty edges/vertices, predetermined by the colors, can be used to construct much better solutions.

Related Work on Colored Graphs.

Faulty colored classes have been used to model Shared Risk Resource Groups (SRRG) in optical telecommunication networks, multi-layered networks, and various other practical contexts; see [11–13] and the references therein. Previous work mainly focused on centralized algorithms for colored variants of classical graph problems (and their hardness). A notable such problem is diverse routing, where the goal is to find two (or more) color disjoint paths between two vertices [14–16]. Another is the colored variant of minimum cut, known also as the hedge connectivity, where the objective is to determine the minimum number of colors (aka hedges) whose removal disconnects the graph; see e.g. [17–19].

A different line of work focuses on distances to or between color classes, and specifically on (centralized) data structures that, given a query \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\langle v, c \right\rangle $$\end{document}v,c, report the closest c-colored vertex to v in the graph, or the (approximate) distance from it [20–25].

Remark on Color Lists.

One might ask what happens if the correlated sets of failures are allowed to have some “bounded” overlap. This can be modeled by color lists: every vertex/edge has a small list of associated colors, and the failure of any one of them will cause its crash. In some problems, the relevant complexity measures might be affected even when lists only have some constant size, see e.g. [10]. However, all the results of the current paper can be easily shown to hold with color lists of constant size, and we therefore focus only on the disjoint color classes model.

Our Results

We initiate the study of fault-tolerant labeling schemes in colored graphs. All of our results apply both to edge-colored and to vertex-colored (multi)-graphs.

Single Color Fault (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f=1$$\end{document}f=1)

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f = 1$$\end{document}f=1, i.e., a single faulty color, we (nearly) settle the complexity of the problem, by showing a simple construction of labels with length \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(\sqrt{n} \log n)$$\end{document}O(nlogn) bits, along with a matching lower bound of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega (\sqrt{n})$$\end{document}Ω(n) bits. In fact, our scheme provides a strong beyond worst-case guarantee: for every given graph G, the length of the assigned labels is (nearly) the best possible, even compared to schemes that are tailor-made to only handle colorings of the topology in G, or equivalently, are allowed to store the uncolored topology “for free” in all the labels. (By the topology of G, we mean the uncolored graph obtained from G by ignoring the colors. Slightly abusing notation, we refer to this object as the graph topology G, rather than the colored graph G.) Guarantees of this form, known as universal optimality, have sparked major interest in the graph algorithms community, and particularly in recent years, following the influential work of Haeupler, Wajc and Zuzic [26] in the distributed setting. On an intuitive level, the universal optimality implies that even when restricting attention to any class of graphs, e.g. planar graphs, our scheme performs asymptotically as well as the optimal scheme for this specific class. We note that one cannot compete with a scheme that is optimal for the given graph and its coloring (aka “instance optimal”), as such a tailor-made scheme may store the entire colored graph “for free”, and the labels merely need to specify the query.

Our universally optimal labels are based on a new graph parameter called the ball packing number, denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{bp}(G)$$\end{document}bp(G). When disregarding minor nuances by assuming G is connected, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{bp}(G)$$\end{document}bp(G) is the maximum integer r such that one can fit r disjoint balls of radius r in the topology of G (see formal definition in Section 3.1). The ball packing number of an n-vertex graph is always at most \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sqrt{n}$$\end{document}n, but often much smaller. For example, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{bp}(G)$$\end{document}bp(G) is smaller than the diameter of G. In Section 3, we show the following:

Theorem 1.1

(\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f=1$$\end{document}f=1, informal) There is a connectivity labeling scheme for one color fault, that for every n-vertex graph G, assigns \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(\textsf{bp}(G)\log n)$$\end{document}O(bp(G)logn)-bit labels. Moreover, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega (\textsf{bp}(G))$$\end{document}Ω(bp(G))-bit labels are necessary, even for labeling schemes tailor-made for the topology of G, i.e., where the uncolored topology is given in addition to the query labels.

The lower bound in Theorem 1.1 is information-theoretic, obtained via communication complexity. The upper bound is based on observing that (when G is connected) there is a subset A of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(\textsf{bp}(G))$$\end{document}O(bp(G)) vertices which is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(\textsf{bp}(G))$$\end{document}O(bp(G))-ruling: every vertex in G has a path to A of length \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(\textsf{bp}(G))$$\end{document}O(bp(G)).

Routing Schemes.

Building upon our labeling scheme, we additionally provide a routing scheme for avoiding any single forbidden color. This is a natural extension of the forbidden-set routing framework, initially introduced by [1] (see also [3–5, 8]), to the setting of colored graphs. We refer the reader to [5] for an overview of forbidden-set routing, and related settings. Such a routing scheme consists of two algorithms. The first is a preprocessing (centralized) algorithm that computes routing tables to be stored at each vertex of G, and labels for the vertices and the colors. The second is a distributed routing algorithm that enables routing a message M from a source vertex s to a target vertex t avoiding edges of color c. Initially, the labels of s, t, and c are found in the source s. Then, at each intermediate node v in the route, v should use the information in its table, and in the (short) header of the message, to determine where the message should be sent; formally, v should compute the port number of the next edge to be taken from v (which must not be of color c). It may also edit the header for future purposes. The main concern is minimizing the size of the tables and labels, and even more so of the header (as it is communicated through the route). We show:

Theorem 1.2

There is a deterministic routing scheme for avoiding one forbidden color such that, for a given colored n-vertex graph G, the following hold:The routing tables stored at the vertices are all of size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(\textsf{bp}(G) \log n)$$\end{document}O(bp(G)logn) bits.The labels assigned to the vertices and the colors are of size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(\textsf{bp}(G) \log n)$$\end{document}O(bp(G)logn) bits.The header size required for routing a message is of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(\log n)$$\end{document}O(logn) bits.

The routing tables stored at the vertices are all of size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(\textsf{bp}(G) \log n)$$\end{document}O(bp(G)logn) bits.

The routing tables stored at the vertices are all of size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(\textsf{bp}(G) \log n)$$\end{document}O(bp(G)logn) bits.

The labels assigned to the vertices and the colors are of size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(\textsf{bp}(G) \log n)$$\end{document}O(bp(G)logn) bits.

The labels assigned to the vertices and the colors are of size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(\textsf{bp}(G) \log n)$$\end{document}O(bp(G)logn) bits.

The header size required for routing a message is of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(\log n)$$\end{document}O(logn) bits.

The header size required for routing a message is of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(\log n)$$\end{document}O(logn) bits.

Another important concern is optimizing the stretch of the routing scheme, which is the ratio between the length of the routing path and the length of the shortest s, t path in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G-c$$\end{document}G-c. Unfortunately, our routing scheme does not provide good stretch guarantees, and optimizing it is an interesting direction for future work. We note, however, that the need to avoid edges of color c by itself poses a nontrivial challenge, and black-box application of the state-of-the-art routings schemes of Dory and Parter [5] for avoiding \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f = \Omega (n)$$\end{document}f=Ω(n) individual edges would yield large labels, tables and headers, and large stretch (all become \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega (n)$$\end{document}Ω(n)).

Centralized Oracles.

We end our discussion for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f=1$$\end{document}f=1 by considering centralized oracles (data structures) for connectivity under a single color fault. In this setting, one can utilize centralization to improve on the naive approach of storing all labels. We note (in Section 3.4) that this problem can be solved using existing O(n)-space and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(\log \log n)$$\end{document}O(loglogn)-query time oracles for nearest colored ancestor on trees [23, 27], yielding the same bounds for single color fault connectivity oracles. Interestingly, our lower bound shows that oracles with such space cannot be evenly distributed into labels.

f Color Faults

It has been widely noted that in fault-tolerant settings, handling even two faults may be significantly more challenging than handling a single fault. Such phenomena appeared, e.g., in distance oracles [28], min-cut oracles [29], reachability oracles [30] and distance preservers [31–33]. In our case, this is manifested in generalized upper and lower bounds on the label length required to support f color faults, exhibiting a gap when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f\ge 2$$\end{document}f≥2; our upper bound is roughly \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(n^{1-1/2^f})$$\end{document}O~(n1-1/2f) bits, while the lower bound is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega (n^{1-1/(f+1)})$$\end{document}Ω(n1-1/(f+1)) bits (both equal \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\Theta }(\sqrt{n})$$\end{document}Θ~(n) when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f=1$$\end{document}f=1).

Theorem 1.3

(\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f \ge 2$$\end{document}f≥2 upper bound, informal) There is a randomized labeling scheme for connectivity under f color faults with label length of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\min \{fn^{1-1/2^f}, n\} \cdot \operatorname {polylog}(fn)$$\end{document}min{fn1-1/2f,n}·polylog(fn) bits.

Theorem 1.4

(\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f \ge 2$$\end{document}f≥2 lower bound, informal) A labeling scheme for connectivity under f color faults must have label length of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega (n^{1-1/(f+1)})$$\end{document}Ω(n1-1/(f+1)) bits for constant f, hence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega (n^{1-o(1)})$$\end{document}Ω(n1-o(1)) bits for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f = \omega (1)$$\end{document}f=ω(1).

The full discussion appears in Section 4. Apart from the gap between the bounds, there are a few more noteworthy differences from the case of a single color fault.

First, the scheme of Theorem 1.3 is randomized, as opposed to the deterministic scheme for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f=1$$\end{document}f=1 (Theorem 1.1). Moreover, the construction is based on different techniques, combining three main ingredients: (1) sparsification tools for colored graphs [10], (2) the (randomized) edge fault-tolerant labeling scheme of [5], and (3) a recursive approach of [7].

Second, the lower bound of Theorem 1.4 is existential (but still information-theoretic): it relies on choosing a fixed ‘worst-case’ graph topology, and encoding information by coloring it and storing some of the resulting labels. We further argue that this technique cannot yield a lower bound stronger than \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\Omega }(n^{1-1/(f+1)})$$\end{document}Ω~(n1-1/(f+1)) bits. This is due to the observation that a color whose label is not stored can be considered never faulty, combined with the existence of efficient labeling schemes when the number of colors is small. A detailed discussion of this barrier appears in Section 4.2.

Curiously, in the seemingly unrelated problem of small-size fault-tolerant distance preservers (FT-BFS) introduced by Parter and Peleg [34], there is a similar gap in the known bounds for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f\ge 3$$\end{document}f≥3, of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(n^{2-1/2^f})$$\end{document}O(n2-1/2f) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega (n^{2-1/(f+1)})$$\end{document}Ω(n2-1/(f+1)) edges [31, 35]. Notably, for the case of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f=2$$\end{document}f=2, Parter [31] provided a tight upper bound of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(n^{5/3})$$\end{document}O(n5/3), later simplified by Gupta and Khan [32]. Revealing connections between FT-BFS structures and the labels problem of this paper is an intriguing direction for future work.

Two Color Faults and Graphs of Bounded Diameter

For the special case of two color faults, we provide another scheme, with label length of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(D \sqrt{n})$$\end{document}O~(Dn) bits for graphs of diameter at most D.

Theorem 1.5

(\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f=2$$\end{document}f=2 (upper bound, informal) There is a labeling scheme for connectivity under two color faults with label length of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(D \sqrt{n})$$\end{document}O~(Dn) bits.

This beats the general scheme when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D = O(n^{1/4 - \epsilon })$$\end{document}D=O(n1/4-ϵ), and demonstrates that the existential \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega (n^{2/3})$$\end{document}Ω(n2/3) lower bound does not apply to graphs with diameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D = O(n^{1/6 - \epsilon })$$\end{document}D=O(n1/6-ϵ). Further, this scheme is existentially optimal (up to logarithmic factors) for graphs with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D= \tilde{O}(1)$$\end{document}D=O~(1). We hope this construction, found in Section 5, could serve as a stepping stone towards closing the current gap between our bounds, and towards generalizing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{bp}(G)$$\end{document}bp(G) for the case of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f=2$$\end{document}f=2.

Equivalence Between All-Pairs and Single-Source Connectivity

In the single-source variant of fault-tolerant connectivity, we are given an n-vertex graph G with a designated source vertex s, and an integer f. It is then required to support queries of the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\langle u,F \right\rangle $$\end{document}u,F, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u\in V$$\end{document}u∈V and F is a faulty-set of size at most f, by reporting whether u is connected to s in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G-F$$\end{document}G-F. Here, and throughout this discussion, we do not care about the type of faulty elements; these could be edges, vertices or colors. For concreteness, we focus our discussion on labeling schemes, although it applies more generally to other models, e.g., centralized oracles, streaming, and dynamic algorithms. Clearly, every labeling scheme for all-pairs fault-tolerant connectivity can be transformed into a single-source variant by including s’s label in all other labels, which at most doubles the label length. We consider the converse direction, and show that a single-source scheme can be used as a black-box to obtain an all-pairs scheme with only a small overhead in length.

Theorem 1.6

(Single-source reduction, informal)

Suppose there is a single-source f fault-tolerant connectivity labeling scheme using labels of at most b(n, f) bits. Then, there is an all-pairs f fault-tolerant connectivity labeling scheme with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(b(n+1, f))$$\end{document}O~(b(n+1,f))-bit labels.

The reduction is based on the following idea. Suppose we add a new source vertex s to G, and include each edge from s to the other vertices independently with probability p. Given a query \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\langle u,v,F \right\rangle $$\end{document}u,v,F, if u, v are originally connected in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G - F$$\end{document}G-F, they must agree on connectivity to the new source s, regardless of p. However, if u, v are disconnected in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G-F$$\end{document}G-F, and p is such that 1/p is roughly the size of u’s connected component in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G-F$$\end{document}G-F, then with constant probability, u and v will disagree on connectivity to s. The full proof appears in Section 6.

Discussion and Future Directions

While our work provides an essentially complete picture for the case of a single color fault, our results for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f\ge 2$$\end{document}f≥2 color faults still leave open many interesting directions for future research:Can we close the gap between the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(n^{1-1/2^f})$$\end{document}O~(n1-1/2f) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega (n^{1-1/(f+1)})$$\end{document}Ω(n1-1/(f+1)) bounds? Concretely, is there a labeling scheme for connectivity under \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f=2$$\end{document}f=2 color faults with labels of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(n^{2/3})$$\end{document}O~(n2/3) bits? Can our solution for low-diameter graphs be utilized to obtain such a scheme?Is there a graph parameter that generalizes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{bp}(G)$$\end{document}bp(G) and characterizes the length of a universally optimal labeling scheme for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f \ge 2$$\end{document}f≥2? Notably, we can show that there are very simple graphs with small diameter and ball packing number, which admit a lower bound of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega (\sqrt{n})$$\end{document}Ω(n) bits for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f=2$$\end{document}f=2 (see Corollary 5.4), so \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{bp}(G)$$\end{document}bp(G) itself does not provide a such a characterization for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f=2$$\end{document}f=2.Can we provide non-trivial centralized oracles for connectivity under \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f \ge 2$$\end{document}f≥2 color faults?Are there routing schemes for avoiding \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f\ge 2$$\end{document}f≥2 forbidden colors with small header size?Our labeling scheme for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f\ge 2$$\end{document}f≥2 could be extended to such a routing scheme, but with a large header size of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(n^{1-1/2^f})$$\end{document}O~(n1-1/2f) bits.Another intriguing direction is going beyond connectivity queries; a natural goal is to additionally obtain approximate distances, which is open even for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f=1$$\end{document}f=1. This problem is closely related to providing forbidden color routing schemes with good stretch guarantees.

Can we close the gap between the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(n^{1-1/2^f})$$\end{document}O~(n1-1/2f) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega (n^{1-1/(f+1)})$$\end{document}Ω(n1-1/(f+1)) bounds? Concretely, is there a labeling scheme for connectivity under \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f=2$$\end{document}f=2 color faults with labels of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(n^{2/3})$$\end{document}O~(n2/3) bits? Can our solution for low-diameter graphs be utilized to obtain such a scheme?

Can we close the gap between the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(n^{1-1/2^f})$$\end{document}O~(n1-1/2f) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega (n^{1-1/(f+1)})$$\end{document}Ω(n1-1/(f+1)) bounds? Concretely, is there a labeling scheme for connectivity under \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f=2$$\end{document}f=2 color faults with labels of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(n^{2/3})$$\end{document}O~(n2/3) bits? Can our solution for low-diameter graphs be utilized to obtain such a scheme?

Is there a graph parameter that generalizes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{bp}(G)$$\end{document}bp(G) and characterizes the length of a universally optimal labeling scheme for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f \ge 2$$\end{document}f≥2? Is there a graph parameter that generalizes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{bp}(G)$$\end{document}bp(G) and characterizes the length of a universally optimal labeling scheme for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f \ge 2$$\end{document}f≥2? Can we provide non-trivial centralized oracles for connectivity under \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f \ge 2$$\end{document}f≥2 color faults?

Are there routing schemes for avoiding \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f\ge 2$$\end{document}f≥2 forbidden colors with small header size?Our labeling scheme for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f\ge 2$$\end{document}f≥2 could be extended to such a routing scheme, but with a large header size of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(n^{1-1/2^f})$$\end{document}O~(n1-1/2f) bits.

Our labeling scheme for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f\ge 2$$\end{document}f≥2 could be extended to such a routing scheme, but with a large header size of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(n^{1-1/2^f})$$\end{document}O~(n1-1/2f) bits.

Preliminaries

Colored Graphs. Throughout, we denote the given input graph by G, which is an undirected graph with n vertices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V = V(G)$$\end{document}V=V(G), and m edges \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E = E(G)$$\end{document}E=E(G). The graph G may be a multi-graph, i.e., there may be several different edges with the same endpoints (parallel edges). The edges or the vertices of G are each given a color from a set of C possible colors. The coloring is arbitrary; there are no ‘legality’ restrictions (e.g., edges sharing an endpoint may have the same color). Without loss of generality, we sometimes assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C \le \max \{m,n\}$$\end{document}C≤max{m,n}, and that the set of colors is [C]. For a (faulty) subset of colors F, we denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G-F$$\end{document}G-F the subgraph of G where all edges (or vertices) with color from F are deleted. When F is a singleton \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F = \{c\}$$\end{document}F={c}, we use the shorthand \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G - c$$\end{document}G-c.

In some cases, we refer only to the topology of the graph, and ignore the coloring. Put differently, we sometimes consider the family of inputs given by all different colorings of a fixed graph. This object is referred to as the graph topology G, rather than the graph G. We denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {dist}_G (u,v)$$\end{document}distG(u,v) the number of edges in a u-v shortest path (and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\infty $$\end{document}∞ if no such path exist). For a non-empty \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A \subseteq V$$\end{document}A⊆V, the distance from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u \in V$$\end{document}u∈V to A is defined as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {dist}_G(u,A) = \min \{\operatorname {dist}_G(u,a) \mid a \in A\}$$\end{document}distG(u,A)=min{distG(u,a)∣a∈A}.

Our presentation focuses, somewhat arbitrarily, on the edge-colored case; throughout, this case is assumed to hold unless we explicitly state otherwise. This is justified by the following discussion.

Vertex vs. Edge Colorings.

An edge-colored instance can be reduced to a vertex-colored one, and vice versa, by subdividing each edge3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e = \{u,v\}$$\end{document}e={u,v} into two edges \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{u,x_e\}$$\end{document}{u,xe} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{x_e, v\}$$\end{document}{xe,v}, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_e$$\end{document}xe is a new vertex.If the original instance is edge-colored, we give the new instance vertex colors, by coloring each new vertex \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_e$$\end{document}xe with the original color of the edge e. (The original vertices get a new “null color”.)If the original instance is vertex-colored, then for each original edge \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e = \{u,v\}$$\end{document}e={u,v} we color the corresponding new edges \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{u,x_e\}$$\end{document}{u,xe} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{x_e, v\}$$\end{document}{xe,v} by the color of the original vertex incident to them, i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{u,x_e\}$$\end{document}{u,xe} gets u’s color, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{x_e, v\}$$\end{document}{xe,v} gets v’s color.These reductions increase the number of vertices in the instance from n to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n + m$$\end{document}n+m, which a prioi might seem problematic. However, as shown by [10], given any fixed (constant) bound f on the number of faulty colors, one can replace a given input instance (either vertex- or edge-colored) by an equivalent sparse subgraph with only \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(n)$$\end{document}O~(n) edges, that has the same connectivity as the original graph under any set of at most f color faults (see Theorem 4.2). So, by sparsifying before applying the reduction, the number of vertices increases only to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(n)$$\end{document}O~(n). We provide the formal details in Section D.

If the original instance is edge-colored, we give the new instance vertex colors, by coloring each new vertex \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_e$$\end{document}xe with the original color of the edge e. (The original vertices get a new “null color”.)

If the original instance is edge-colored, we give the new instance vertex colors, by coloring each new vertex \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_e$$\end{document}xe with the original color of the edge e. (The original vertices get a new “null color”.)

If the original instance is vertex-colored, then for each original edge \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e = \{u,v\}$$\end{document}e={u,v} we color the corresponding new edges \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{u,x_e\}$$\end{document}{u,xe} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{x_e, v\}$$\end{document}{xe,v} by the color of the original vertex incident to them, i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{u,x_e\}$$\end{document}{u,xe} gets u’s color, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{x_e, v\}$$\end{document}{xe,v} gets v’s color.

If the original instance is vertex-colored, then for each original edge \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e = \{u,v\}$$\end{document}e={u,v} we color the corresponding new edges \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{u,x_e\}$$\end{document}{u,xe} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{x_e, v\}$$\end{document}{xe,v} by the color of the original vertex incident to them, i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{u,x_e\}$$\end{document}{u,xe} gets u’s color, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{x_e, v\}$$\end{document}{xe,v} gets v’s color.

Moreover, we note that all of the proofs given in this work can in fact be converted directly and rather seamlessly between the edge-colored and the vertex-colored cases, even without using these general reductions.

Vertex and Component IDs.

We assume without loss of generality that the vertices have unique \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(\log n)$$\end{document}O(logn)-bit identifiers from [n], where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{id}(v)$$\end{document}id(v) denotes the identifier of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v \in V$$\end{document}v∈V. Using these, we define identifiers for connected components in subgraphs of G, as follows. When \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G'$$\end{document}G′ is a subgraph of G and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v \in V(G')$$\end{document}v∈V(G′), we define \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{cid}(v,G') = \min \{\textsf{id}(u) \mid u,v\text { connected in }G'\}$$\end{document}cid(v,G′)=min{id(u)∣u,vconnected inG′}. This ensures \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{cid}(u,G') = \textsf{cid}(v,G')$$\end{document}cid(u,G′)=cid(v,G′) iff u, v are in the same connected component in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G'$$\end{document}G′. Therefore, if one can compute \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{cid}(v,G-F)$$\end{document}cid(v,G-F) from the labels of v, F, then, using the same labels, one can answer connectivity queries subject to faults.

Indexing Lower Bound.

Our lower bounds rely on the classic indexing lower bound from communication complexity. In the one-way communication problem \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsc {Index}(N)$$\end{document}INDEX(N), Alice holds a string \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x \in \{0,1\}^N$$\end{document}x∈{0,1}N, and Bob holds an index \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in [0,N-1]$$\end{document}i∈[0,N-1]. The goal is for Alice to send a message to Bob, such that Bob can recover \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_i$$\end{document}xi, the i-th bit of x. Crucially, the communication is one-way; Bob cannot send any message to Alice. The protocols are allowed to be randomized, in which case both Alice and Bob have access to a public random string. The following lower bound on the number of bits Alice is required to send is well-known (see [36–38]).

Lemma 2.1

(Indexing Lower Bound [37]) Every one-way communication protocol (even with shared randomness) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsc {Index}(N)$$\end{document}INDEX(N) must use \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega (N)$$\end{document}Ω(N) bits of communication.

Single Color Fault

In this section, we study the connectivity problem under one color fault. That is, given two vertices u, v and a faulty color c, one should be able to determine if u, v are connected in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G-c$$\end{document}G-c. In Sections 3.1 and 3.2 we focus on labeling schemes, and provide universally optimal upper and lower bounds. Section 3.3 then discusses routing in the presence of a single forbidden color. In Section 3.4 we change gears and provide centralized oracles for the problem.

Our Labeling Scheme and the Ball Packing Number

We first show a scheme that works when G is connected. Connectivity cannot be assumed without losing generality, because of the color labels: A color gets only one label, which should support connectivity queries in every connected component of the input. Later, in Appendix A, we show how to remove this assumption. Consider the following procedure: starting from an arbitrary vertex \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_0$$\end{document}a0, iteratively choose a vertex \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_i$$\end{document}ai which satisfies\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \operatorname {dist}_G ( a_i, \{a_0, \dots , a_{i-1}\} ) = i, $$\end{document}distG(ai,{a0,⋯,ai-1})=i,until no such vertex exists. Suppose the procedure halts at the k-th iteration, with the set of chosen vertices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A = \{a_0, \dots , a_{k-1}\}$$\end{document}A={a0,⋯,ak-1}. Then every vertex \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v \in V$$\end{document}v∈V has distance less than k from A.
